
**What the document says:** Louvois sends the provisions of governor of Exilles. "L'intention de Sa Majesté est qu'aussitost que le lieu que vous aurez jugé propre audit Exiles pour garder seurement les deux prisonniers de la tour d'en-bas, sera en estat de les recevoir, vous les fassiez sortir de la citadelle de Pignerol dans une litière, et les y fassiez conduire sous l'escorte de vostre compagnie", with the enclosed march orders; immediately after their departure Saint-Mars is to go to Exilles and reside there. Because the King does not want "le reste des prisonniers qui estoient à vostre garde, lesquels doivent rester dans la citadelle de Pignerol" left to a battalion captain who may change daily, an order is enclosed to have the sieur de Villebois recognised as commandant of the citadel until Rissan returns or another is appointed; Duchaunoy has orders to pay Villebois "deux escus par jour, pour la nourriture de ces trois prisonniers". The company is reduced to 45 men from the 15th, with the statement of pay and "ce que le roy vous a ordonné pour la subsistance des susdits deux prisonniers", whom the King expects him to guard with the same exactitude; news from time to time. "A l'esgard des hardes que vous avez au sieur Matthioli, vous n'avez qu'à les faire porter à Exiles pour les luy pouvoir rendre, si jamais Sa Majesté ordonnoit qu'il fût mis en liberté."

**English (key sentences):** "His Majesty's intention is that as soon as the place you will have judged proper at the said Exilles to guard securely the two prisoners of the lower tower is in a state to receive them, you have them leave the citadel of Pignerol in a litter and have them conducted there under the escort of your company... the sieur du Chaunoy has orders to pay [Villebois] two écus a day for the food of these three prisoners... With regard to the effects of the sieur Matthioli that you have, you have only to have them carried to Exilles so as to be able to return them to him, if ever His Majesty should order him to be set at liberty."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Lair and Funck-Brentano: 2 + 3 = five prisoners; the three left are Mattioli, the Jacobin and Dubreuil, the two taken are La Rivière and Dauger (Lair), or Dauger and Dubreuil (Funck-Brentano as reported by Mongrédien; but Funck-Brentano p. 118-119 lists the five as La Rivière, the Jacobin, Dubreuil, Dauger, Mattioli). Iung: the three left are Eustache Danger, Dubreuil and Mattioli (with valet), the two taken the Jacobin and the 1674 prisoner. Topin: the sentence on Matthioli's effects "a naturellement confirmé chacun dans l'opinion du transport de Matthioly à Exiles" until the 25 June letter proved otherwise. Ellis: an "additional proof" that Matthioli went to Exilles. Iung (p. 154): 2 écus a day = 2,190 livres a year each for the two of Exilles; Villebois "n'eut que 2 livres" — Iung reads the 2 écus as the allowance for the two Exilles prisoners, whereas Delort's text attaches the 2 écus to Villebois "pour la nourriture de ces trois prisonniers" (see money section).

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Date 9 June (Delort, Iung, Ellis, Lair, Funck-Brentano) vs "8 juin 1681" (Topin p. 329). "deux escus par jour... ces trois prisonniers" (Delort) — Ellis: "two crowns a day, for the support of those three prisoners"; Iung applies the 2 écus to the Exilles pair. "hardes" (Delort copy 1 OCR "bardes"; Topin "bardes").

**OCR notes:** Delort copy 1 lacks the opening of the letter (p. 269 top missing); copy 2 supplies it. "Yillebois" = Villebois; "l>atail-lon" = bataillon; "Ghannoy" = Chaunoy.

*Source: Ellis, The True History of the State Prisoner commonly called the Iron Mask (1826) — file `ellis1826_true_history_state_prisoner.txt`, lines 11141–11171 — Ellis 1826, Appendix No. 113 (English)*

```text
No. 113.
LOUVOIS TO SAINT-MARS.

Versailles, June 11th, 1681.
I HAVE acquainted the King with the contents
of your letter of the 13th of last month, and with
the list of the repairs necessary to be made to the
tower at Exiles, which you deem the most proper
residence for the prisoners whom His Majesty
leaves under your care. The King has thought
fit to grant you a thousand crowns, as well for
the aforesaid repairs, as for those which you shall
judge necessary to make in your own lodging;
which, as soon as you receive this, you will take
care to have done immediately, as if the expense
was to come out of your own pocket : and as soon
as the prison shall be in a fit state, it is the inten-
tion of His Majesty that you should transfer the
aforesaid two prisoners to it, according to what I
have already commanded you in my last letter;
and in conformity with that and the order which
was joined to it, you will then deliver to the Sieur



S26 THE IRON MA^K.

de Villebois the command of the citadel of Pig-
nerol.

De Louvois.*
```

**What the document says:** Louvois has reported Saint-Mars's letter "du dernier du mois passé" and the memoir of repairs to the tower of Exilles which he judges fit "à mettre les prisonniers que Sa Majesté laisse à vostre garde"; the King grants 1,000 écus for these repairs and those to his lodging, the work to proceed at once "comme si cette despense se faisoit à vos despens"; when the prison is ready the two prisoners are to be transferred as ordered, and the command of the citadel handed to Villebois.

**English (key sentences):** "The King has thought fit to grant you a thousand écus, both for the said repairs and for those you will judge proper to make to your lodging; to which, by means of that, you will take care to have work done at once, as if this expense were made at your own expense; and when the prison is in a state, His Majesty's intention is that you transfer the said two prisoners to it..."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Ellis: the pretence of paying himself was "for the purpose of concealing from the neighbourhood, that any prisoners of importance were to be removed".

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Delort: "vostre lettre du dernier du mois passé"; Ellis: "your letter of the 13th of last month".


### E81 — 25 June 1681 — Saint-Mars to the abbé d'Estrades (Turin): "j'aurai en garde deux merles que j'ai ici, lesquels n'ont point d'autres noms que messieurs de la tour d'en bas; Matthioli restera ici avec deux autres prisonniers"

- Sender / recipient: Saint-Mars (Pignerol) to the abbé d'Estrades
- Archive reference given by the editions: Ravaisson: "(B. I.)" (Bibliothèque impériale, manuscrits d'Estrades); Topin: "en minute parmi les manuscrits d'Estrades à la Bibliothèque impériale"; Lair: "Ms. d'Estrades, Bibl. nat."
- Printed in: Ravaisson t. III, pp. 214-215 (full, with a long note); Topin 1883, pp. 329-331 (extract to "soumis serviteur"); Lair p. 478; Funck-Brentano p. 118; Iung p. 326 (paraphrase, "de juin 1681"); Laloy p. 125; Mongrédien p. 94 (snippet).


**What the document says:** Saint-Mars apologises for not having announced his government of Exilles; "J'ai reçu hier seulement mes provisions de gouverneur d'Exilés avec deux mille livres d'appointement; l'on m'y conserve ma compagnie franche et deux de mes lieutenants, et j'aurai en garde deux merles que j'ai ici, lesquels n'ont point d'autres noms que messieurs de la tour d'en bas; Matthioli restera ici avec deux autres prisonniers. Un de mes lieutenants, nommé Villebois, les gardera, et il a un brevet pour commander en mon absence à la citadelle et au donjon, jusqu'à ce que M. de Rissan revienne"; Saint-Martin has the majority of Montlouis with 700 écus and Blainvilliers that of the citadel of Metz; "Je ne crois pas partir d'ici que devers la fin du mois qui vient"; he will go to Exilles from time to time for repairs; he must settle there for the winter "avec toute ma famille et les ours"; he asks to be recommended to Their Royal Highnesses and has never obtained leave to go to Turin.

**English (key sentences):** "I received only yesterday my provisions as governor of Exilles with two thousand livres of salary; they keep for me my free company and two of my lieutenants, and I shall have in my keeping two blackbirds that I have here, who have no other names than the gentlemen of the lower tower; Matthioli will remain here with two other prisoners. One of my lieutenants, named Villebois, will guard them..."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Topin (1883) uses this letter to prove Mattioli stayed at Pignerol; Ravaisson's note: Saint-Mars stayed to keep Mattioli and "l'emmena à Exiles vers la fin de septembre ou le commencement d'octobre" (contradicted by the letter itself); Ravaisson's note to the 27 September letter says he took the two prisoners to Exilles "le 12 juillet suivant" (the date of Saint-Mars's letter E83, not of the transfer). Lair: the merles are La Rivière and Danger. Iung: the Jacobin and his cell-mate. Mongrédien: discussion of the word "merles". Money: 2,000 livres of appointements here vs the 500 livres a month of E78 (Lair p. 478 says "500 livres par mois").

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Ravaisson: "deux merles que j'ai ici, lesquels n'ont point d'autres noms que messieurs de la tour d'en bas"; Topin: "d'autres noms"; Lair: "deux merles que j'ay icy, lesquels n'ont point d'autre nom que Messieurs de la Tour d'en bas"; Funck-Brentano: "d'autre nom". Iung says Saint-Mars wrote "à l'abbé d'Estrades... de juin 1681" that he would leave "avec deux merles".

**OCR notes:** Ravaisson scan: "doQJon" = donjon; "la un du mois" = la fin du mois; "Matliioli" = Mattioli.

*Source: Ellis, The True History of the State Prisoner commonly called the Iron Mask (1826) — file `ellis1826_true_history_state_prisoner.txt`, lines 11205–11240 — Ellis 1826, Appendix No. 115 (English)*

```text
No. 115.

SAINT-MARS TO LOUVOIS.

Precautions for the Security and Concealment of tlie

Prisoners at Exiles.

, Pignerol, July 12th, 1681.
Just setting off for Exiles.

In order that the prisoners may not be seen (at
Exiles,) they will not leave their chamber when
they hear mass; and in order that they may
be kept the more securely, one of my lieutenants
will sleep above them, and there will be two sen-
tinels night and day, who will watch the whole
round of the tower, without its being possible for
them and the prisoners to see and to speak to one
another, or even to hear any thing of one another*
They will be the soldiers of my company, who will
be always the sentinels over the prisoners. There is
only a confessor, about whom I have my doubts ;
but if you do not disapprove, I will give them
the curate of Exiles instead, who is a good man,
and very old, whom I will forbid, on the part of His



8528 THE lEON MASK.

Majesty, to enquire who these prisoners are, or
their names, or what they have been, or to speak
of them in any way, or to receive from them by
word of mouth, or by writing, .eitlier communica^
tions or notes.
```

**What the document says:** So that the prisoners are not seen at Exilles they will not leave their room to hear mass; for greater security one of his lieutenants will sleep above them, and two sentinels day and night will see the whole round of the tower without the prisoners and they being able to see, speak or even hear each other; soldiers of his company will always be posted; only a confessor worries him: if Louvois agrees he will give them the curé of Exilles, a good and very old man, whom he will forbid on the King's behalf to know who these prisoners are, their names, what they have been, ever to speak of them, or to receive any communication or note by word or writing.

**English (key sentences):** "So that the prisoners are not seen (at Exilles), they will not leave their room to hear mass; and to keep them in greater security, one of my lieutenants will sleep above them, and there will be two sentinels day and night who will see the whole round of the tower, without them and the prisoners being able to see or speak to one another, nor even hear... There is only a confessor who worries me a little; but if you judge it proper, I will give them the curé of Exilles..."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Ravaisson's note that Saint-Mars "mena les deux prisonniers à Exiles le 12 juillet" misreads the heading "au moment d'aller à Exiles" (Saint-Mars went to inspect; the 22 July letter shows the prisoners still at Pignerol and the departure postponed to October).

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Delort's heading: "De Pignerol, le 12 juillet 1681, au moment d'aller à Exiles"; Ellis: "Just setting off for Exiles".

**OCR notes:** Delort copy 1 p. 273 is garbled: "le ïa juillet 1681, moment d^aller à Eiileg" = le 12 juillet 1681, au moment d'aller à Exiles; "sùvelé" = sûreté; "raxï^" = nuit.

**What the document says:** Louvois sees by the letter of the 12th that the repairs at Exilles will not let Saint-Mars leave Pignerol before the end of next month; as the King's service may require him to stay the following month as well, he should hurry the repairs little enough to have a pretext for not leaving until about the first days of October, "observant de vous conduire de manière qu'il ne paroisse point d'affectation au séjour que vous y ferez"; the order for reimbursing the expense made for his prisoners will follow; a packet for M. de Pianesse.

**English (key sentences):** "...it will be well that you press the said repairs of Exilles little enough for you to have a pretext for leaving Pignerol only about the first days of October, taking care to conduct yourself so that no affectation appears in the stay you will make there."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Iung: the delay was caused by Duchaunoy's illness and by the Casale enterprise (Catinat's secret return); "Dès le 22 juillet, et non pas le 2 août, comme l'a prétendu M. Marius Topin, Louvois écrivait à Catinat".

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** —

**What the document says:** 3 Aug.: the King allows Saint-Mars to visit the marquis de Pianesse at his country house and go to Turin if he wishes, provided he does not sleep out of the citadel more than one night in a row; as to the Exilles journey and the leave asked for the sieur Tourtebat, the King does not intend him to go there.

With regard to the journey to Exilles, and the leave you ask for the sieur Tourtebat, whom you would like to take with you, you will have seen by my preceding letters that the King's intention is not that you go there."

**What the document says:** The King has ordered M. de Catinat to go at once to Pignerol for the same affair that took him there at the beginning of 1679; Saint-Mars is to prepare a lodging where he can stay hidden three weeks or a month; when Catinat sends word that he has arrived at the place where Saint-Mars went to find him in 1679, Saint-Mars is to fetch him into the donjon with all precautions so that no one knows he is with him; packets from Piedmont or Italy for him are to be kept.

**English (key sentences):** "The King having ordered M. de Catinat to go at the first opportunity to Pignerol for the same affair that had taken him there at the beginning of the year 1679, I write these lines by His Majesty's order to give you notice so that you prepare him a lodging in which he can stay hidden for three weeks or a month..."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Iung: this explains "la fameuse lettre... du 20 septembre 1681" (E90) — "le dernier prisonnier que vous avez entre les mains" being Catinat, as Saint-Mars's own marginal note says.

**What the document says:** Catinat arrived on the 3rd; he is called Guibert, an engineer sent by the King's order, from Nice; he had himself arrested beyond Pignerol on the Pancalier road; Saint-Mars keeps him prisoner in all forms, with a profusion of admirable figs.

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Iung: key to the "dernier prisonnier" of 20/30 September (E90).

**What the document says:** 27 Sept.: thanks; he has given d'Estrades's letter to Catinat, who leaves tomorrow, Sunday, with the infantry, and is to be governor of the citadel [of Casale]; as soon as Saint-Mars can leave Pignerol, where he still has some affairs, he will come to Turin and then go "en mon exil d'Exilés"; a mountain merchant troubled by the Susa customs. 29 Sept.: joy at the success of the affair d'Estrades put on the way (Casale).

**English (key sentences):** "As soon as I can leave here, where I still have some affairs concerning the service, I will come to assure you of my obedience... and then from there I will go to my exile of Exilles."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Ravaisson's note (on the 25 June letter) fixes the departure for Exilles "vers la fin de septembre ou le commencement d'octobre"; Iung: "vers le milieu d'octobre, après la prise de Casal"; the letters of 27-29 September show the prisoners still at Pignerol on those dates.


**What the document says:** Acknowledges the letter of the 16th; the King will not take it ill that Saint-Mars goes from time to time to see "le dernier prisonnier que vous avez entre les mains", once he is established in his new prison; and as soon as he has left the one where Saint-Mars holds him, the King wishes Saint-Mars to execute the order for his establishment at Exilles; a packet for M. de Richemont. Saint-Mars's note: "Ce nom veut dire M. de Catinat, que je tenois pour lors enfermé à Pignerol."

**English (key sentences):** "The King will not take it ill that you go from time to time to see the last prisoner you have in your hands, when he is established in his new prison, and as soon as he has left the one where you keep him, His Majesty desires that you execute the order she sent you for your establishment at Exilles."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Delort printed the letter with Saint-Mars's note; earlier writers (Roux-Fazillac) took "le dernier prisonnier" for Mattioli. Iung dates it 20 September and calls the note an autograph in K 120.

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Date: 30 September (Delort) vs 20 September (Iung). The "nouvelle prison" of Catinat = Casale (Iung).

**OCR notes:** Delort copy 1: "3o septembre 1681" = 30 septembre; "estabf^r ^ns" = estably dans; the note: "3f. de Cotawi" = M. de Catinat; "enferma" = enfermé.

```text
1  P.  232,  V.  654,  Mss.  Dépôt  de  la  guerre.
```

**What the document says:** No letter printed in these editions records the day of the transfer. The 9 June order prescribes a litter and the escort of the company; the 22 July letter fixes the pretext of leaving "vers les premiers jours du mois d'octobre"; Saint-Mars was still at Pignerol on 27-29 September; Louvois's letter of 30 September tells him to execute the Exilles order once Catinat has left; the next dated letter from Exilles printed is of 11 March 1682 (E95), and Topin quotes a Louvois letter of 14 December 1681 addressed to Saint-Mars "établi à Exiles" (E92).

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Ravaisson's "12 juillet" is an error (see E83). Iung's "milieu d'octobre" and Ravaisson's "fin de septembre ou commencement d'octobre" are inferences from the Casale chronology.

**What the document says:** Saint-Mars may have his prisoners clothed, but the clothes must last three or four years "à ces sortes de gens-là".

**English (key sentences):** "You may have your prisoners clothed... But the clothes must last three or four years for that sort of people."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Roux-Fazillac (1801) draws from it that the masked man was one of these two "sortes de gens" and that the fine clothes of the legend vanish; Topin and Lair use it to show the low condition of the two merles.

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Roux-Fazillac: "Il faut, écrivoit Louvois, que les habits durent trois ou quatre ans à ces sortes de gens-là"; Topin: "Vous pouvez faire habiller vos prisonniers... Mais il faut que les habits durent trois ou quatre ans à ces sortes de gens-là".

**What the document says:** 9 Jan.: M. de Saint-Mars has been at Turin since yesterday, lodging this time with M. de Masin. 16 Jan.: the sieur Caluzio, questioned by the marquis de Pianezze, has a very bad reputation; he treats with Rosetti for the masonry of the fortifications of Casale.

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Ravaisson's notes: Saint-Mars's liberties show the masked man was not at Exilles (his reasoning); Caluzio "ne fut pas mis à Pignerol" (contradicted by E27 and Roux-Fazillac's list).

**What the document says:** 2 March: "Comme il est important d'empêcher que les prisonniers qui sont à Exiles, que l'on nommoit à Pignerol de la Tour d'en bas, n'aient aucun commerce, le Roi m'a ordonné de vous commander de les faire garder si sévèrement... qu'ils ne parleront à qui que ce soit, non-seulement de dehors, mais même de la garnison d'Exiles". 11 March: Saint-Mars has kept "ces deux prisonniers" as severely and exactly as Foucquet and Lauzun; they can hear people passing on the road at the foot of the tower but cannot make themselves heard; they can see people on the mountain in front of their windows but cannot be seen because of the grilles; two sentinels of his company night and day on both sides of the tower see the windows obliquely and are ordered to listen whether anyone speaks to them and whether they shout, and to move on passers-by; his own room adjoins the tower; inside, a "tambour" covering their double doors prevents the priest saying mass from seeing them; the servants put the food on a table and his lieutenant carries it in; no one speaks to them but himself, his officer, M. Vigneron the confessor, and a physician from Pragelas six leagues away, in his presence; linen and other necessities with the same precautions as for his past prisoners.

**English (key sentences):** "...I have guarded these two prisoners in my custody as severely and exactly as I formerly did MM. Foucquet and Lauzun, who cannot boast of having given or received any news as long as they were shut up. These can hear people speak who pass on the road at the foot of the tower where they are; but they, even if they wished, could not make themselves heard; they can see people on the mountain in front of their windows, but they cannot be seen, because of the grilles in front of their rooms... No one speaks to them but me, my officer, M. Vigneron (the confessor), and a physician from Pragelas, six leagues from here, and in my presence."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Delort's note identifies the two as Lestang/Matthioli and the Dominican/Jacobin (his identification, disproved by E81 for Mattioli). Lair reads them as La Rivière and Danger. Iung as the Jacobin and the 1674 prisoner.

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Delort prints Saint-Mars's letter with "le 27 du passé" as the date of Louvois's letter; Iung dates Louvois's letter 2 March 1682 and says Saint-Mars answered "à cette question et à une autre du 27 février".

**OCR notes:** Delort copy 1 pp. 280-281 badly OCR-ed ("daux prisoqniersuqui sotkX, à, oaa g%r4e" = deux prisonniers qui sont à ma garde).

**What the document says:** Louvois has received Villebois's letter of the 10th on the trouble Dubreuil gives; if he continues to play the madman he is to be treated like people who have lost their wits, "c'est-à-dire le bien étriller"; when he asked to speak to Saint-Mars, Villebois should have said Saint-Mars still commanded in the donjon.

**English (key sentences):** "If he continues to play the madman, you have only to treat him as people who have lost their minds, that is to say give him a good thrashing, and you will see that this will bring him back to his senses."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Proves Dubreuil stayed at Pignerol in 1682 (Iung, Lair, Laloy agree). Iung's list of the three left at Pignerol: Eustache Danger, Dubreuil, Mattioli (with valet).

```text
mais  il  est  plus  que  probable  que  notre  homme  fut  simple-
```

**What the document says:** It is long since Saint-Mars spoke of his prisoners; Louvois asks how he governs them and how they are, and what he knows of the birth of "le nommé la Rivière" and of the adventure by which he was put in the service of the late M. Foucquet.

**English (key sentences):** "It is a long time since you spoke to me of your prisoners. I beg you to tell me how you govern them and how they are. Tell me also what you know of the birth of the man named La Rivière and of the adventure by which he was put in the service of the late M. Foucquet."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Iung reads it as Louvois asking Saint-Mars, "alors à Exiles, des nouvelles de ceux qu'il a laissés à Pignerol" (his reading; the letter says "vos prisonniers"); Lair and Laloy read it as proof La Rivière was at Exilles with Saint-Mars. Ravaisson's note: La Rivière "avait été enfermé avec Foucquet, pour être son domestique".

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Iung: "Il y a longtemps que vous ne m'avez parlé de vos prisonniers..."; Ravaisson: same text.

**What the document says:** Lair: in June 1685 one prisoner wants to make his will; 3 January 1686 one "Messieurs" becomes hydropique; 3 November 1686 Louvois allows confession "lorsque vous verrez apparence d'une prochaine mort. Jusque-là, il ne faut pas que luy ou son camarade aient aucune communication"; "Le 4 janvier 1687, l'hydropique était mort"; Saint-Mars had only one prisoner left, Eustache Danger. Barbezieux, 13 August 1691: "Lorsque vous aurez quelque chose à me mander du prisonnier qui est sous vostre garde depuis vingt ans, je vous prie d'user des mesmes précautions que vous faisiez quand vous les donniez à M. de Louvois."

**English (key sentences):** "When you have something to tell me about the prisoner who has been in your custody for twenty years, I beg you to use the same precautions you used when you gave them to M. de Louvois."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Lair: "depuis vingt ans" fits Danger (1669) not La Rivière (1666); Topin: it fits the Jacobin (1674, "dix-sept ans") as a round number; Laloy (p. 125): the Jacobin probably died before 11 January 1694 at Pignerol (he places him among the three left). These are the editors' inferences; the death of January 1687 is not attributed by name in any letter printed here.

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Death date: 4 January 1687 (Lair) / end of December 1686 (Funck-Brentano, citing Lair II, 479) / January 1687 (Iung, for the Jacobin).

```text
Saint-Mars  parti  de  Pignerol ,  Eustache  Danger  se  trouva
le  plus  ancien  des  prisonniers  du  donjon.  Il  végéta  ainsi  treize
années  encore,  ignoré  même,  comme  tant  d'autres,  de  son
nouveau  geôlier,  le  sieur  de  La  Prade,  et  de  Barbezieux.  Il  fut
de  ceux  qui,  de  même  que  Mattioli  etDubreuil,  devaient  être

delle, le  malheureux  détenu  fut  trouvé  mort  dans  sa  prison ,
un  matin  des  premiers  jours  de  janvier  1694.  La  Prade  r.e

était  le  plus  ancien  des  misérables  confiés  à  sa  garde.  Il  fît
```

*Source: Laloy, Le Masque de fer: Jacques de La Cloche, l'abbé Prignani, Roux de Marsilly (1913) — file `laloy1913_masque_de_fer_lacloche_prignani_marsilly.txt`, lines 4516–4531 — Laloy, p. 126*

```text
17  sept.  1680.  Comme  nous  ne  connnaissons  aucune
entrée  de  celte  date  au  9  juin  1681,  quoique  la  corres-
pondance de  ces  années  soit  bien  conservée,  il  est  bien
probable  qu'il  a  survécu  jusqu'au  9  juin  1681  au  moins.
Dans  ce  cas,  Userait  mort  vraisemblablement  avant  le
11  janvier  1694,  car  à  cette  date,  Barbezieux  écrit  à
Saint-Mars  :  «  Le  sieur  de  La  Prade,  à  qui  le  Roi  a
confié  la  garde  des  prisonniers  qui  sont  détenus  par
ordre  du  Roi  dans  le  donjon  de  Pignerol,  m'écrit  que

MM.  Loiseleur  et  Topin,  se  basant  sur  les  expressions
```

**What the document says:** La Prade, to whom the King entrusted the prisoners in the donjon of Pignerol, writes that the oldest is dead and that he does not know his name; Barbezieux, not doubting Saint-Mars remembers it, asks him to send it in cipher. Laloy: Saint-Mars answered on 28 January, but his answer is not preserved.

**English (key sentences):** "The sieur de La Prade, to whom the King has entrusted the guard of the prisoners detained by the King's order in the donjon of Pignerol, writes to me that the oldest is dead and that he does not know his name. As I do not doubt that you remember it, I beg you to send it to me in cipher."

**Editorial inferences and glosses (kept separate from the text):** Iung: "Le plus ancien est Eustache Dauger" (died at Pignerol, January 1694 — his chapter heading). Laloy: the dead man is probably the Jacobin. Lair: Dauger was at Exilles, then the Islands, then the Bastille (d. 1703). The letter names no one.

**Variant readings and dates across editions:** Date 16 January (Iung) vs 11 January 1694 (Laloy).


## 2. Prisoner roster at Pignerol, 1665-1681

Sources for the table: Iung's chapter headings (IUN pp. 187-260, "Historique des prisonniers", quoted in E02, E04, E23, E35, E46, E76 and reproduced in section 2c), Laloy pp. 118-127, Lair pp. 436-485, Ravaisson t. III, Delort 1829/1825, Roux-Fazillac pp. 23-26 and 37-40, Topin pp. 307-309, Funck-Brentano pp. 118-119. "Doc." = date given by a printed document; "Ed." = date given only by an editor. Where editions disagree the alternatives are listed.

| Prisoner (names used in the letters) | Arrival at Pignerol | Departure / death | Where he was kept; what the letters say about him | Money per prisoner (as printed) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Nicolas Foucquet, "monsieur Foucquet" | 16 January 1665 (Roux-Fazillac p. 23: "Fouquet entra... le 16 janvier"; Lair) | died at Pignerol 23 March 1680 (Doc.: reply of 8 April 1680, E67; Laloy "subitement"); body released to his family 8-9 April 1680 (E68) | tour d'angle, 2nd floor (Laloy p. 115); two valets from 1667; regime softened Nov. 1677 and Jan. 1679 (E41); secret hole to Lauzun's room discovered 23 March 1680 | 500 livres a month for his food (Iung p. 154, "jusqu'en 1681"); 15,000 livres gratification to Saint-Mars 30 Jan. 1679 (E42) |
| Foucquet's first valets (unnamed) | with Foucquet 1665; one fell ill at La Pérouse and was replaced 15 Oct. 1665 (Laloy/Iung) | freed when cured (orders of 15 Oct. and 12 Dec. 1665; 23 Sept. 1666 rule of 3-4 months' retention) | 600 livres wages ordered 24 Dec. 1664 (Laloy p. 118) | 600 livres a year wages (1664) |
| Champagne, "le nommé Champagne, valet de monsieur Foucquet" | chosen 21 April 1667 (Laloy; Iung says the two 1667 valets were "Champagne et Honneste") | died "la Notre-Dame de septembre" (8 Sept.) of the year before Foucquet's letter (E34): 1674 per Lair and Mongrédien; "En 1675" per Laloy; "mis à la porte" per Iung | in "prison dure" from 17 Dec. 1669 (E10); not returned to Foucquet 11 Feb. 1670 (E14); back with Foucquet by 1674 (E34) | wages withdrawn 16 Jan. 1670 (E12); 50 livres a month wages plus 50 livres food per valet (Iung p. 202, undated) |
| La Rivière, "le nommé la Rivière, autre valet de monsieur Foucquet"; later one of the "deux de la tour d'en bas" per Lair/Laloy | 21 April 1667 (Laloy); "vraisemblablement depuis 1666" (Lair p. 481 n.); after 1670 (Iung) | shut up with Eustache from 8 April 1680 (E67); taken to Exilles autumn 1681 (Lair, Laloy, FB, Mongrédien — not Iung); died at Exilles 4 Jan. 1687 (Lair) / end Dec. 1686 (FB) [Iung: died at Pignerol? — Iung leaves him unresolved, p. 203] | 17 Dec. 1669: may be left with Foucquet or removed with 7-8 months' prison; 1679 asks his wages (E62); 8 April 1680 to be reported "mis en liberté"; 16 April 1684 Louvois asks his birth and how he came to serve Foucquet (E97) | wages withdrawn 1670; 50 livres a month as valet (Iung; Lair "50 francs par mois" from April 1680) |
| "Eustache d'Auger" (D29 19 Jul. 1669; LAL 21 Aug.), "Eustache Danger" (D29 15 Feb. 1679; RAV 17 Feb. 1679; LAI), "Eustache Dauger" (RAV 13 Sept. 1679; IUN), "Eustache d'Angers" (D29 8 Apr. 1680), "le nommé Eustache" (23 Dec. 1678, 20 Jan. 1679, 10 Jul. 1680), "le prisonnier que M. de Vauroy vous a amené", "vostre nouveau prisonnier", "le prisonnier de la tour", "l'homme de la tour", "votre second prisonnier" | order to Vauroy 28 July 1669 (Doc.); handed over by Vauroy: Saint-Mars's letter of 21 Aug. 1669 (Laloy) / arrival "20 août" (Roux-Fazillac, Topin) / "24 août" (Mongrédien; cf. Louvois acknowledging letters "des 24 et dernier du mois passé", E07) / "écroué le 28 juillet" (FB, conflating order and entry) | Lair, Laloy, FB, Mongrédien: taken to Exilles 1681 (one of the two merles), to Sainte-Marguerite 1687, to the Bastille 1698, d. 19 Nov. 1703. Iung: stayed at Pignerol, "le plus ancien", died January 1694 (E100). ### 2a. What the letters say about the number of prisoners

- 10 September 1669: "vostre nouveau prisonnier" (E07); 31 October 1670: "votre second prisonnier et les valets de M. Foucquet" (E21) — two state prisoners (Foucquet and the new one) plus valets.
- 20 February 1672: "ce prisonnier qui est dans la tour"; "l'homme de la tour" (E26).
- 30 December 1673: Foucquet, Lauzun, "le prisonnier de la tour", Buticari, Castanieri (E28).
- 2 May 1676: "le dernier prisonnier qui vous a été envoyé" — the prisoner of 1674 or the Jacobin (E35).
- 8 April 1680: Lauzun; "les nommés Eustache d'Angers et le dit la Rivière" to be shut up together; "les autres prisonniers dont vous estes chargé" at 4 livres a day each (E67). Laloy's count for April 1680: Lauzun, the Jacobin, Dubreuil, Dauger, La Rivière, Matthioly, Matthioly's valet (seven).
- 10 July 1680: "les habitans de la tour d'en bas"; Lestang; "le nommé Eustache" (E69). Iung's count on that day: six (Lauzun and valet; Eustache Danger [with La Rivière]; Mattioli and valet; Dubreuil; the Jacobin and the unknown prisoner).
- 11 May 1681: "deux des prisonniers qui sont à sa garde" to go to Exilles (E77). 12 May 1681: "les deux prisonniers de la tour d'en-bas, qui sont, je crois, les seuls que Sa Majesté fera transférer"; a memoir of all prisoners requested (E78) — the memoir itself is not printed in any edition consulted.
- 9 June 1681: "les deux prisonniers de la tour d'en-bas" (to Exilles); "le reste des prisonniers... lesquels doivent rester dans la citadelle de Pignerol"; "deux escus par jour, pour la nourriture de ces trois prisonniers" (E79) — five in all (Lair, Funck-Brentano, Laloy).
- 25 June 1681: "deux merles que j'ai ici, lesquels n'ont point d'autres noms que messieurs de la tour d'en bas; Matthioli restera ici avec deux autres prisonniers" (E81) — again five, Mattioli among the three who stay.
- 11 March 1682: "mes deux prisonniers", "ces deux prisonniers qui sont à ma garde" at Exilles (E95); 23 December 1685: "Mes prisonniers" (E98); January 1687: one dead (E99); 1691: "le prisonnier qui est sous vostre garde depuis vingt ans" (E99).
- 24 May 1682: Dubreuil at Pignerol under Villebois (E96); 1694: "le plus ancien" of the Pignerol prisoners dead (E100).

### 2b. Money per prisoner (as printed)

- Foucquet: 500 livres a month for food (Iung p. 154, "jusqu'en 1681" — i.e. until 1680); Lauzun 600 livres a month (Iung); valets 50 livres a month wages plus clothing, and 50 livres a head for food (Iung p. 202); 600 livres a year wages fixed 24 Dec. 1664 (Laloy); wages withdrawn 16 Jan. 1670 (E12); La Rivière's arrears asked 4 Sept. 1679 (E62).
- Dauger 1669: furniture and food reimbursed, "ce n'est qu'un valet" (E03).
- "Les autres prisonniers": 4 livres a day each, statement every three months (8 April 1680, E67); Iung converts it to 120 livres a month and says Eustache and La Rivière stayed at the valet rate (50 livres a month = "une livre cinquante par jour"), an inference.
- "Vingt sous par jour" for the 1674 prisoner and Dubreuil: Roux-Fazillac p. 26 ("la dépense de l'un et de l'autre ne doit pas excéder vingt sous par jour") and Topin p. 308; the order itself is not printed.
- 30 Jan. 1679: 15,000 livres gratification to Saint-Mars (E42).
- 12 May 1681: the Exilles provisions "ne passant pas quatre mille livres"; the King continues "les mesmes cinq cents livres par mois" he gave at Pignerol (E78). 25 June 1681: "mes provisions de gouverneur d'Exilés avec deux mille livres d'appointement" (E81). 11 June 1681: 1,000 écus for repairs (E80). 22 July 1681: reimbursement of "la dépense que vous avez faite pour vos prisonniers" (E84).
- 9 June 1681: Duchaunoy to pay Villebois "deux escus par jour, pour la nourriture de ces trois prisonniers" (E79). Iung p. 154 reads the two écus as the rate for each of the two Exilles prisoners ("2190 livres pour chacun par an") and gives Villebois "2 livres" for those of the donjon; Delort's text attaches the two écus to the three left at Pignerol.
- 14 Dec. 1681: clothes to last three or four years (E92); 1687: the Exilles prisoner's bed and effects sold for thirteen écus (Topin p. 332, Lair p. 480).

Each heading gives the arrest/entry and exit/death dates as Iung prints them (Iung 1873, "Historique des prisonniers", pp. 187-260 and 305-360).

## 3. Discrepancies between editions

1. Reference of the 19 July 1669 letter: Iung "P. 272, v. 234, Mss. Dépôt de la guerre" (a folio of the 28 July minutes) vs Laloy "K 120 A" autograph of Louvois and Lair "K, 120, 67" (E03).
2. Name in the 19 July letter: Delort/Laloy "le nommé Eustache d'Auger"; Iung and Barnes "le nommé X..." / "le nommé..." — Laloy calls this an error of Iung and Barnes (E03).
3. The 28 July 1669 orders: three separate minutes (Laloy, f° 271, 272, 274) vs two orders, one addressed jointly "à MM. de Saint-Mars et marquis de Piennes" (Iung); Iung dates the courier's arrival at Dunkerque "1er août 1669"; Funck-Brentano writes "écroué à Pignerol le 28 juillet 1669" (E04). Mongrédien's snippet prints the name "Eustache Dauger" in the orders where Laloy and Iung leave a blank.
4. Arrival at Pignerol / Saint-Mars's first report: 21 August 1669 (Laloy), 20 August (Roux-Fazillac, Topin), 24 August (Mongrédien; and Louvois's acknowledgement of letters "des 24 et dernier du mois passé", E07), "juillet 1669" (Ravaisson's note, E19), 28 July (Funck-Brentano) (E05).
5. Date of the prayer-book letter: 10 September 1669 (Delort, Laloy, Iung's footnote "10 septembre 1669, K, 120") vs "Le 27 septembre" in Iung's text (E07).
6. Iung runs the 10 September 1669 letter and a paraphrase of 26 March 1670 (Honneste) together as one quotation, and drops "ou un des valetz de monsieur Foucquet" (E07, E15).
7. 12 April 1670: "pour ma sûreté" (Delort, Laloy) vs "pour sa sûreté" (Iung) (E16).
8. 17 December 1669: Lair's footnote calls it "Louvois à Foucquet" (it is to Saint-Mars); Lair "Laforêt / Honeste" vs Delort "La Forest / Honneste"; Roux-Fazillac "Valcroissant" (E10).
9. Iung's statement that the two valets of 1667 were "Champagne et Honneste" and that "Champagne fut mis également à la porte" (p. 202) vs Laloy (Champagne and La Rivière, 21 April 1667) and Foucquet's letter on Champagne's death (E34).
10. Champagne's death: September 1674 (Lair's dating of Foucquet's letter; Mongrédien "En 1674") vs "En 1675" (Laloy) vs "mis à la porte" (Iung) (E34).
11. La Rivière's arrival: 21 April 1667 (Laloy) vs "vraisemblablement depuis 1666" (Lair p. 481 n., citing 23 Sept. 1666 and 17 Sept. 1669) vs after 1670 (Iung); Lair p. 481 "venu à Pignerol en 1666" (E34, E99).
12. Date of the approval of Dauger as Foucquet's valet: 30 January 1675 (Delort, Laloy, Lair) vs "31 janvier 1675" (Iung); Iung prints the postscript as part of the body (E32).
13. When Dauger became Foucquet's valet: 1675 (Lair, Laloy, Funck-Brentano, Mongrédien) vs after 27 December 1678 / January 1679 (Iung) — Lair p. 464 note says Iung erred (E32, E39, E41).
14. 27 December 1678: Ravaisson's text (release of Lauzun's valet with three years' wages) vs Iung's text of the same date ("Sa Majesté profiteroit de l'avis... au sujet du prisonnier que le sieur de Vauroy lui avoit amené", P. 524, v. 681) vs Laloy's "ordre du 1er décembre 1678" with "cent écus" (E39).
15. 23 December 1678 Louvois to Foucquet: "adoucissements fort considérables" (Ravaisson, Lair) vs "adoucissements considérables" (Laloy); Ravaisson marks a lacuna after "du papier et" (E37).
16. Lair's footnote "Louvois à Saint-Mars, 24 décembre 1679. Arch. nat. K, 120, 245. Delort, Détention, t. I, p. 273" for the cover letter that Delort prints at p. 279 dated 23 December 1678 (E37, E38).
17. Lair's footnote "17 février 1669" for Louvois to Foucquet of 17 February 1679 (E45).
18. Spelling of the name by letter: 19 Jul. 1669 "d'Auger" (D29, LAL); 21 Aug. 1669 "d'Auger" (LAL); 15 Feb. 1679 "Eu stache Danger" (D29) / "Eustache Dauger" (LAL, LAI) / "Eustache Danger" (IUN); 17 Feb. 1679 "Eustache Danger" (RAV, IUN); 13 Sept. 1679 "Eustache Dauger" (RAV) / "Eustache Danger" (LAL); 8 Apr. 1680 "Eustache d'Angers" (D29, LAL) / "Eustache Dauger" (IUN) / "Eustache Danger" (LAI); 10 Jul. 1680 "le nommé Eustache" (D25) / "le nommé Eustache Dauger" (Mongrédien snippet). Lair p. 453 n.: "Le nom se lit de manières différentes: Danger, Dauger, d'Anger, d'Auger, d'Angers" (E03, E44, E45, E63, E67, E69).
19. 4 September 1679 letter on La Rivière's wages: printed only by Iung ("P. 82, v. 624") (E62).
20. Mattioli's arrest: 2 May 1679 ("hier" in Catinat's letter of 3 May; Iung's heading) — other dates (3 May, 5 May) in the secondary literature derive from the letter date; valet arrested 4 May (Iung) (E46).
21. 8 April 1680: Iung once calls it "la lettre du 6 avril 1680" (E67); Topin's footnote reads "12 mars 1680" for the 12 May 1681 request of a memoir (OCR "12nRS:lG80", E30 block).
22. Who occupied the tour d'en bas after April 1680 and who were "les deux prisonniers de la tour d'en bas" / "deux merles" of 1681: Dauger and La Rivière (Lair, Laloy, Funck-Brentano, Mongrédien); the Jacobin and the 1674 prisoner (Iung); Mattioli and the Jacobin (Roux-Fazillac, Delort's note to 11 March 1682, Ellis); Dauger and Dubreuil (Funck-Brentano as reported by Mongrédien's snippet p. 155 — but FB pp. 118-119 lists five with Dubreuil dying at Sainte-Marguerite). Lair p. 477 puts Mattioli and the Jacobin in the tour d'en bas in 1680 while also making La Rivière and Danger its "habitants" (E67, E69, E70, E78, E81, E95).
23. Who stayed at Pignerol in 1681: Mattioli, Dubreuil and the Jacobin (Lair, Laloy, Topin, FB); Eustache Danger, Dubreuil, Mattioli and valet (Iung); Ravaisson's note: Saint-Mars stayed "afin de garder Mattioli... il l'emmena à Exiles" (E81).
24. 12 May 1681: Iung's two readings "P. 232, v. 654" (p. 163) and "P. 232, v. 684" (p. 397); Ellis's text "May 13th" vs his appendix "May 12th"; Delort "K. 129" (E78).
25. 9 June 1681: Topin "8 juin 1681"; Ellis's No. 113 refers to Saint-Mars's letter "of the 13th of last month" where Delort has "du dernier du mois passé" (E79, E80).
26. The two écus a day of 9 June 1681: for the three left at Pignerol, paid to Villebois (Delort's text, Ellis, Lair, FB) vs for each of the two Exilles prisoners, "2190 livres pour chacun par an", Villebois "2 livres" (Iung p. 154) (E79).
27. Saint-Mars's Exilles pay: "les mesmes cinq cents livres par mois" (12 May, E78; Lair "500 livres par mois") vs "deux mille livres d'appointement" (25 June, E81; and the 5 January 1682 letter comparing Casale at 2,000 livres, E93).
28. 12 July 1681: Ravaisson's note "Il mena les deux prisonniers à Exiles le 12 juillet suivant" vs the letter's heading "au moment d'aller à Exiles" and the 22 July / 30 September letters showing the departure postponed to October (E83, E84, E90, E91).
29. Date of the transfer to Exilles: "vers la fin de septembre ou le commencement d'octobre" (Ravaisson) / "vers le milieu d'octobre, après la prise de Casal" (Iung) / 12 July (Ravaisson's other note); no document printed gives the day (E91).
30. 30 September 1681 (Delort) vs "20 septembre 1681" (Iung) for the "dernier prisonnier" letter; Saint-Mars's note printed by Delort in K 129, called by Iung an autograph note in K 120 (E90).
31. 7 September 1680 (Delort, Iung) vs "17 sept. 1680" (Laloy) for the Matthioli-Jacobin letter (E71).
32. 2 March 1682 (Iung) vs "le 27 du passé" (Saint-Mars's acknowledgement in Delort) for Louvois's Exilles order; Iung says Saint-Mars answered both a 2 March and a 27 February letter (E95).
33. Death of the first Exilles prisoner: 4 January 1687 (Lair, La Rivière) / "fin de décembre 1686" (FB, citing Lair) / "janvier 1687" (Iung, the Jacobin) (E99).
34. The Jacobin's death: January 1687 at Exilles (Iung) / before 11 January 1694 at Pignerol (Laloy) / "fin de 1693" (FB, citing Revue historique LV, 1894, p. 294) (E35, E99, E100).
35. Barbezieux's letter on "le plus ancien": 16 January 1694 (Iung, "P. 128, v. 1242") vs 11 January 1694 (Laloy citing Iung p. 328) (E100).
36. The 1674 prisoner: order "10 mars 1674" (Ravaisson, Laloy) vs "Ordre d'envoi au donjon de Pignerol, 20 mars 1674" (Iung heading); arrival "7 avril 1674" (Iung, Topin) is a computation; identity: Oldendorf/the masked man (Iung) vs the Jacobin (Laloy, Topin, Roux-Fazillac) (E29-E31).
37. Dubreuil: arrest order 25 Feb. 1676 (Iung) / arrested 24 April 1676 near Basle (Laloy) / "emprisonné en juin 1676" (Topin, Roux-Fazillac); death: Sainte-Marguerite, date unknown (Iung) / transferred 1694 (Lair) / "vers 1697" (FB).
38. Dauger's end: died at Pignerol January 1694 (Iung heading and p. 196) vs Exilles-Sainte-Marguerite-Bastille, d. 1703 (Lair, Laloy, FB, Mongrédien) (E100).
39. Delort 1829's closing note "il peut en voir les originaux aux Archives du Royaume, Section Historique, K. 129" for the letters that Lair and Laloy cite as K 120 / K 120 A; Delort 1825 cites "K. 129" for the 1679-1687 letters (E03, E78).
40. Caluzio: "ne fut pas mis à Pignerol" (Ravaisson's note, E94) vs Iung's heading (two days in the citadel, Sept. 1673) and Topin's 14 September 1673 letter (E27); Roux-Fazillac identifies Caluzio with Buticary, Topin separates them.
41. Vauroy's title: "capitaine de Vauroy, sergent-major de ma ville et citadelle de Dunkerque" (28 July orders) / "le major de Dunquerque" (26 March 1670) / "M. le major de Dunkerque" (20 Feb. 1672) / Lair "major de la citadelle de Dunkerque" / Iung "le major de la citadelle".
42. Ravaisson's note to 5 September 1671 "Dauger, conduit à Pignerol en 1660?" (misprint for 1669) (E22).
43. The 10 July 1680 letter: Iung says that "par la même lettre de juillet 1680, Louvois recommande de mettre [Eustache] avec le nommé La Rivière" — that order is in the 8 April 1680 letter, not in the 10 July letter (E69).
44. Lauzun's release: 22 April 1681 (Iung heading, Maupertuis's receipt, Topin, Ravaisson's note) vs "au mois de mars de l'année suivante" (Iung p. 326) (E76).

## 4. Texts searched for and not found; sources not obtained

- Saint-Mars's letters of 24 and 31 August 1669, 4 September 1669, 27 November and 8, 19, 21 December 1669, 11 January, 1 February, 8 and 15 March 1670, 19 January and 26 February 1675, 17 December 1678, 7 January, 4 February, 2 September and 4 October 1679, 23 March, 4 July, 7 August, 26 and 27 October, 27 November 1680, 3 May, 31 May, 29 June, 12 and 23 July, 13 and 16 September 1681 — all acknowledged in Louvois's replies; only the extracts noted in the entries (21 and 31 August 1669, 12 April 1670, 7 August 1671, 20 February 1672, 30 December 1673, 24 February, 7 September, 9 and 26 October 1680, 12 July 1681) are printed. Searched by date strings ("aoust 1669", "août 1669", "septembre 1669", etc.) in all files listed in section 0.
- The "mémoire de tous les prisonniers dont vous estes chargé" asked for on 12 May 1681: not printed in any edition consulted (Topin p. 308 says the minister "avait oublié la cause" of their detention; Iung p. 196 refers to "la fameuse liste, où les deux merles ne devaient être marqués que d'un astérisque" — an inference from the 12 May order).
- Foucquet's answer to Louvois's letter of 23 December 1678: not preserved (Lair p. 464).
- Louvois's letter of 14 July 1670 (Lair "K, 120, 83"): cited only (E20).
- Louvois's letters of 13 January 1677 and Saint-Mars's of 8 December 1676 on the Jacobin: cited by Laloy and Iung, not printed (E35).
- The letter of 17 September 1680 cited by Laloy (p. 125-126): not found under that date in Delort 1825, Iung, Ravaisson, Lair or Topin; the letter of 7 September 1680 (E71) matches Laloy's description.
- Louvois's "refus catégorique" to the 20 February 1672 proposal (Iung p. 192): no such letter is printed in any edition consulted; Laloy calls the answer "certainement négative".
- Roux-Fazillac 1801: obtained from numelyo (epub text + 476 per-view OCR pages) and saved to `sources/`; the epub text is the better reading of the two.
- Mongrédien 1952 (*Le Masque de fer*): full text not obtained (Gallica ark bpt6k3357084j is snippet-only through ContentSearch; the snippet file `mongredien_1952_snippets.md` was used for the readings cited as "Mongrédien (snippet)"). Mongrédien's 1953 article in *XVIIe siècle* (nos 17-18, "deux documents inédits") is in `sources/` (`mongredien1953_xviie_siecle_17-18.txt`) but concerns Louis XIV's letter on the princess of Savoy and the Sainte-Marguerite period, not Pignerol 1669-1681.
- Petitfils 1970/2003-2004: only the Open Library / Google Books snippets in `petitfils_2004_snippets.md` and the summary in `dabos_masque-de-fer.com_la_these_eustache_danger_summary_of_petitfils.txt`; archive.org lending item `lemasquedeferent0000peti` blocked for search-inside (HTTP 403).
- Barnes 1908 (`barnes1908_man_of_the_mask.txt`) is in `sources/` but was not used for transcriptions (he depends on Iung and Lair; Laloy's remarks on his errors are quoted in E03).
